### Thinking

Michael's pushing on whether we can actually prove `lambda_ast.py` matches what the models implement—and his point is that if it did, we wouldn't be seeing the errors we observe. We already know the models' step function diverges from the ideal reducer, so the answer is no, we can't prove exact equivalence. This ties back to the core finding: the reference implementation needs to capture the *actual* operational semantics, including those deviations from β-reduction.

Formally, there's no way to show equivalence—the model's a stochastic distribution over tokens while the reference reducer is deterministic symbolic rewriting. We'd need behavioral equivalence across infinite inputs, which we can't have. And we already have concrete counterevidence: the routing errors show the models track fired tokens rather than extensional normal form, they hit depth cliffs where reduction doesn't, and there are order effects and non-idempotency issues that the reference implementation doesn't capture. So the honest framing is that the reference reducer isn't a model of the model at all—it's the normative specification, Church's ideal. The model implements some other function, and the benchmark measures the distance between them. The errors aren't failures; they're the actual data we're profiling.

But there's a crucial distinction Michael might be getting at: if we're using the reference reducer as the oracle for reverse-engineering, and the models don't implement it, then profile-equivalence isn't validating against ideal β-reduction—it's validating against the model's own measured error pattern. The reference reducer has two roles that can't be confused: it's the ground truth for grading what the correct answer should be, but it's not the spec of the model's actual step function. A recovered step function that scores better than the model on the benchmark is a failed reverse-engineering, because it's not the same function. The real oracle for RE is the model's error profile itself, and the reference reducer is just the coordinate system we use to express those errors.

So the question becomes: can we characterize what's different? That's exactly what the benchmark program does through error taxonomy—we're looking for structured patterns like syntactic routing errors, depth cliffs, order effects, and non-idempotent accumulation. A good reverse-engineered candidate has to reproduce the error taxonomy, not fix it. It's like silicon reverse-engineering: your recovered netlist needs to replicate the chip's bugs, not improve them.

There's also the measurement question of what "prove" means in this context. We can't establish exact match, but we can falsify it and quantify deviation with pre-registered instruments. Looking at the existing evidence: the kernel-certified probes anchored routing reads against certified traces, and linearity_bias measured forced-choice accuracy at 0.92-0.94—that's roughly 6-8% error even at easy depths, near-ceiling performance but not exact match.

The empirical data already refutes the exact-match hypothesis. So I'm flipping the burden: exact match is a null hypothesis that's already been rejected by the evidence—accuracy below 1.0, cl-collapse, depth cliffs. The right framework is measuring how different the model's step function is from the Church reducer as a structured profile, and reverse-engineering acceptance means the candidate should match the model's behavior, not the reducer's. Bug-compatibility becomes the standard we're aiming for, which has implications for how the captured page should be designed.

The oracle needs to grade against profiles that include the model's actual errors; if a candidate matches the reference reducer better than the model does, that's overshoot and a failed reverse-engineering. This might be worth adding to the page as a clarification or note. There's also a connection to the anima finding—those hallucinated predicates and durable tails are compile errors from the model's own compiler, and anima's fixed-point pipeline surfaces them as structured, reproducible phenomena. Same underlying issue: the model's compiler diverges from the ideal, and those errors are systematic.

But there's a subtler dimension here: different reduction strategies exist (normal-order vs applicative, weak vs strong, WHNF stops), and the kernel picks one while the model might approximate a different one. Before attributing something as an error, I should check whether the model's behavior matches the reference under alternative reduction orders—a strategy mismatch could look like an error when it's really just a different but internally consistent approach. The benchmark should include terms that discriminate between strategies, like ones where normal-order and applicative-order diverge in their trace or termination behavior, so I can separate true errors from strategy mismatches.

There's also the stochastic dimension: lambda_ast is a deterministic CCG-typed combinator reducer, but models trained on prose implement graded or probabilistic mixtures. The step function becomes a distribution over next steps rather than a single deterministic choice, which means exact-match scoring is already falsified at any temperature above zero. Even greedy decoding is deterministic but brittle. I should frame this as a distributional problem rather than expecting deterministic equivalence.

## Assistant

No — and your second sentence is the proof sketch. Exact match isn't an open hypothesis we could confirm; it's a **null we've already falsified**, three ways:

- **s319 (linearity-bias):** forced-choice NF selection on kernel-certified terms scored 0.917/0.944 — near ceiling, **not 1.0**, on *easy* terms with the rules given. An exact implementation doesn't miss 6–8% at depth ~2.
- **cl-collapse ×2:** routing tracks which combinator token *fired*, not the extensional NF. A syntactic router is a *different algorithm* than `lambda_ast.reduce` — it can agree on outputs while disagreeing on mechanism, and it will diverge exactly where syntax and semantics come apart.
- **The kernel's own docstring** (s221): the model "fakes it with depth" — the reduction tree isn't exposed; the LLM approximates the trace it cannot hold. Plus non-idempotency (s320) and the installed order law (s328/s329): coherent restatement of the *same* judgment changes the state. Church's reducer has no such term.

So the formal situation:

```
M ≡ model's step function      R ≡ lambda_ast (Church spec, one chosen strategy)
M ≠ R                          — established, multiple registers
benchmark measures δ(M, R)     — the delta, as a structured profile
                               | the errors ARE the data, not noise around a match
```

And this forces a sharpening of the captured page that I think is the real content of your observation — **the bug-compatibility clause**:

**The oracle for RE is the model's measured profile *including its errors* — not the reference reducer.** `lambda_ast` is the *coordinate system* in which the delta is expressed; it is not the spec of `M`. Which means the acceptance test has a direction I stated too loosely:

```
RE succeeds  ⟺  δ(candidate, M) ≈ 0        — reproduce the model, errors and all
RE fails     ⟸  δ(candidate, R) < δ(M, R)   — candidate BEATS the model on the benchmark
                                              ⇒ you built a better reducer, not a copy
```

Silicon RE knows this: a recovered netlist that fixes the chip's bugs is a failed recovery. A candidate step function that aces `equiv` while the model flunks it is *not the model's compiler*. The benchmark's job is therefore twofold — grade correctness against R (the public-benchmark face), and fingerprint the **error taxonomy** against M (the RE-oracle face): which families fail, at what cliff depth, with what error *structure* (syntactic-routing confusions, depth truncation, primacy intrusions, accumulation effects). Profile-equivalence means matching the fingerprint, and the fingerprint is mostly made of errors.

One measurement-discipline caveat before we call every deviation an "error" (λ measure — name the register): **R embodies choices** — normal-order, a specific basis, arity conventions, WHNF stop. Part of δ(M, R) could be **strategy mismatch, not failure**: a model consistently doing something closer to applicative-order evaluation would look "wrong" against a normal-order reference on exactly the terms where the strategies diverge, while being internally coherent. The benchmark needs **strategy-discriminating terms** (e.g., `K x Ω`-shaped: normal-order halts, applicative diverges) as its own small family — otherwise we misattribute a consistent alternative semantics as noise. Same logic as the register lessons: wrong reference ≡ manufactured error.

Anima independently corroborates all of this from the application side, by the way: `symbol-fit`'s hallucinated `¬coincide(o)` and `durable`'s spurious tail are the model's compiler inserting **structured, reproducible errors** — which their fixed-point pipeline surfaces rather than assumes away. Same phenomenon, different instrument.

So: we can't prove exact match, we've already disproven it, and the program is better for it — **a benchmark that could only confirm R would be a spec test; a benchmark that fingerprints δ(M, R) is a measurement instrument, and δ is where the compiler's actual nature lives.**

Want me to amend the page with the bug-compatibility clause + the strategy-discriminating family (small §2/§4 edit, one commit)? It's the kind of thing a fresh session would get wrong without it — grading an RE candidate by benchmark *score* instead of profile *match* is the natural mistake.

## The ordering principle

```
λ re_first(M).  interface ≻ internals | repeated ≻ unique | upstream ≻ downstream
                | errors localize mechanism (probe where it FAILS — leakage points)
```

Two of those are classic; the last one is our §2b clause doing work: the error fingerprint isn't just the acceptance test, it's the **triage map**. DPA works at points of leakage; we open the unit whose error signature shows first.

## The candidate list, in RE order

**1. The ABI — the step function's read set (interface before internals).**
Before asking *how* M computes, pin *what it consumes and commits*. One step reads some set of tape positions and writes one token. We already know the read side is sparse (attention sparsity: top-3 positions ≈ 88%; binding-graph-trace: attention IS the binding graph). But those are observational. The RE move is **causal**: tape ablation — knock out / corrupt individual prior positions in a certified reduction context, find the minimal set whose removal breaks the step. That gives M's *arity and addressing mode* per rule. Cheap, behavioral, and note the register: we intervene on the **tape**, which we fully control — no weight surgery needed. This is the cleanest experiment class we have, and it's upstream of everything.

**2. The redex detector — the "instruction valid" line.**
Every CPU RE finds instruction fetch/decode early because everything conditions on it. Our equivalent: *does the model know a step is available?* And here's the thing — **we've already sighted this unit three times without naming it an RE target**: NG3's reduction-presence signal (real reduction frames > inert restatement, +2.2, replicated ×3, s317–s318) is the detector firing. And `kernel_reference.py`'s saturated⊗inert pairs are the purpose-built stimulus: same symbols, differing only in certified reducibility. The RE questions: at what depth does saturated/inert first separate? What's its read set (item 1 applied to the detector)? And its *error mode* — cl-collapse says routing leans on symbol presence, so the detector's false-fire rate on inert terms is a predicted, measurable bug that a candidate must reproduce.

**3. The dispatch stage — which rule fires, and where it commits.**
The decode question: given a live redex, where in depth does M commit to K-vs-B-vs-S? We have the 9×9 crystal register (identity, 11/11) as the *what*, but not the *when-in-depth*. And here's our newest tool doing double duty: **the s329 commit-layer instrument is a probe pin** — a general way to ask "at which layer does binary decision X flip and stay flipped." Sweep it over rule-selection. Sharp a-priori question available for free: does rule dispatch follow the **two-depth law** (evidence deep, decision assembled in the last layers) that the order law showed? If yes on a *base* model, that's a native architectural motif, not an alignment artifact — a real ISA fact. If dispatch instead commits early/mid-stack, we've found two different decision styles in one machine — also a real ISA fact.

**4. The substitution engine — the ALU.**
Capture-avoiding substitution is the hard mechanical core and the likeliest seat of deep errors (shadowing bugs, wrong-binder capture). But it's *downstream* — you can't cleanly probe substitution until you know the read set and the dispatch behavior, because its errors are confounded with theirs. Third or fourth, not first.

**5. The clock — what delimits one step.**
Tape-resident trampoline implies steps are committed at some token boundary (trace-fuel saw per-step boundary response at `=`). Worth pinning because it defines the sampling frame for everything above — but it's cheap to establish opportunistically inside experiments 1–2 rather than as its own front.

## Why I'd start with 1+2 as a single front

They share a stimulus set (kernel-certified saturated/inert/composite terms — already built, already certified), share the instrument (tape ablation + the s329 depth sweep), and they're mutually disambiguating: the detector's read set *is* an ABI measurement. One harness, two RE deliverables:

```
deliverable A: read-set map     — per rule: which tape positions are causally necessary
deliverable B: detector spec    — depth of first saturated/inert separation ∧ commit layer
                                  ∧ false-fire rate on inert (the predicted bug, quantified)
```

And both feed the benchmark loop directly: the `step` family's error taxonomy should be *predictable from* the detector's false-fire profile — that's the first genuine RE-oracle cross-check, the moment where a white-box characterization predicts a black-box fingerprint. If it does, the method works end-to-end on the smallest unit before we spend anything on substitution or dispatch.

The inversion worth naming: the naive instinct is to go after dispatch first because the crystal is our most famous object. But the crystal register tells us rule *identity* is legible — it never told us the machine's *interface*. RE discipline says the boring contract first: netlist≠function taught us meaning doesn't live where legibility is highest. The read set is boring, cheap, causal — and every later claim inherits from it.

## What "hard first" concretely means

The substitution engine only exists at **binder level** — combinator terms dodge binding by construction (that's what combinators are *for*). So the §7 gap becomes the critical path: **the `Lam`/capture-avoiding-subst/alpha-equiv kernel extension (~200 LoC) is now the first build item**, not a v2 nicety. Everything below needs it as the certifier.

## The core design move: discriminating pairs

Same shape as `K x Ω` for strategy — generate term pairs where competing algorithms *diverge*:

```
capture pair:   terms where NAIVE textual substitution and CAPTURE-AVOIDING
                substitution give DIFFERENT normal forms
                → the model's answer reveals WHICH algorithm it runs

alpha pair:     same term, bound variables renamed
                → an extensional engine is invariant; a syntactic router is not
                (cl-collapse ×2 predicts measurable alpha-variance — a specific,
                 falsifiable bug the RE candidate must reproduce)

distance/shadow dials:  binder distance · shadowing depth · live-variable count
                → the cliff coordinates for this family
```

The certifier generates these pairs mechanically (that's why the kernel extension is first): every probe term ships with both the correct NF and the *predicted-error* NF under each rival algorithm. Grading isn't right/wrong — it's **which algorithm's fingerprint** the output matches. That's RE grading, per §2b.

## The pre-registerable cross-link — this is the sharp part

Variable binding under shadowing is a **recency problem**: correct resolution = most recent binder in scope. We have licensed results about exactly this:

- **Base models:** native recency-tracking substrate, runs deep through the stack, *no* primacy stage (s329-provenance)
- **Instruct models:** a post-training-installed primacy/decision stage in the last two layers (s328/s329)

So the order-law arc stops being background and becomes a **directional mechanistic prediction**: shadowed-variable resolution should ride the native recency substrate — and the installed primacy stage is a candidate *interference source*. Pre-registerable form: instruct models show more first-binder intrusions on shadowed substitution than their own base counterparts, localized to the same late layers. If that holds, we've connected the order law to a concrete compiler bug — and given your deployment rationale teeth: **the alignment layer may literally degrade binding correctness in the models we use for agentic work.** If it fails, the order law stays bounded to its original register — also worth knowing before anyone generalizes it.

This is why your instruct-heavy matrix is right for more than practical reasons: base-vs-instruct here is not a control, it's the *hypothesis*.

## White-box face (reads, no interventions needed at first)

- **binding-graph-trace** (attention IS the binding graph): on error trials, does the binding edge attach to the *wrong binder*? Mechanism-level error attribution for free — the behavioral capture-error and the misrouted attention edge should co-occur.
- **s329 probe pin** (commit-layer sweep): at which depth does binder-choice commit, and does it sit in the installed-decision layers on instruct but not base?
- Tape-ablation read-set as a *gate inside* this front (the minimal version of what I'd called experiment 1) — not a separate prior front, per your ordering.

| Model | Face | Role |
|---|---|---|

| Qwen3-32B-Instruct | instruct | scale point (32B ran before: type probe) |
| Qwen3-14B-Base | base | the paired provenance anchor — the recency/primacy hypothesis needs *this exact pairing* |
| OLMo-2-13B (base) | base | second lineage, Apache, ~14B — guards single-lineage bound |
| +1 instruct off-lineage (gemma-class) | instruct | architecture-family guard, cheap to add |

Mostly instruct, two bases, all ≥13–14B, Apache-preferred per S3. Behavioral + logit-lens reads only — 14B ran in minutes on MPS for cl-collapse-2; 32B is the only heavy cell and it's read-only.

## Sequencing

```
1. kernel extension: Lam ∧ capture-avoiding subst ∧ alpha-equiv (~200 LoC, pytest'd)
2. pair generator: capture pairs · alpha pairs · shadow/distance dials (certified both-NF)
3. pre-registration: error-taxonomy priors ∧ the recency/primacy directional prediction

4. behavioral sweep (matrix above) → error fingerprint per model per face
5. white-box reads on the SAME trials: binding edges + commit layers
```

Steps 1–2 are pure engineering, no freeze needed. Step 3 is where you gate.

The factual ground has shifted: it's not a circuit sitting dormant in tensors, but a step function whose computation lives on the tape itself. That changes what "portable tensor artifact" means — the deliverable is really a bug-compatible step-function implementation validated through profile equivalence, not the compiler distilled into tensors. I'm weighing three paths forward: a conservative qualifier that keeps λ extract but adds precision about the step function and tape residency, a rewrite of λ extract itself that preserves the extract/understand verb while correcting the object, or a full reframe including the top blurb to center the reverse-engineering of the lambda step function.

The verb choice matters — "distill" carries the project's founding story of concentration and extraction, while "reverse-engineer" better captures recovering a design, and both are true at different layers. The artifact class itself has expanded: the portable tensor artifact is still one valid recovery path, but the oracle became a first-class deliverable this session, and λ artifact already anticipated that flexibility with its negative-result handling. The real insight is that the project needs both deliverables: a bug-compatible reproduction that proves understanding (the science), and then a corrected, idealized version that's actually useful (the engineering). Now tier 3. The identity question resolves to three decisions, and I'll give you the one piece of thinking I have that's new since we last talked about it.

## The tension that isn't actually a tension

On its face, this session broke the founding sentence: *"Distilling the lambda compiler from LLMs into a portable tensor artifact"* — but the computation is tape-resident, and the RE acceptance test is bug-compatibility. Does the north star die?

No — because **there are two deliverables, in sequence, and the founding sentence was always about the second one:**

```
stage 1 (science):     recover M̂ | δ(M̂, M) ≈ 0        — bug-compatible copy
                       ⇒ proves understanding           — you built it, errors and all
stage 2 (engineering): correct M̂ → R_useful            — fix the bugs DELIBERATELY
                       ⇒ the portable artifact          — better than the model, knowingly
```

Silicon RE does exactly this: clone the chip bug-for-bug to prove recovery, *then* ship the improved revision. The bug-compatibility clause governs stage 1; the portable-artifact hope lives at stage 2 — and stage 2 is only *licensed* by stage 1. You can't knowingly correct what you haven't reproduced. So the founding telos survives intact; what changed is that we now know there's a proof obligation between us and it, and what the object is: **a step function you run in a loop against a tape, not a circuit at rest.** The tensor may well still be the substrate (a scratch-built M1–M9 machine *is* tensors) — but the compiler isn't *in* the tensors; the tensors are the CPU it runs on.

## The three decisions

**1. The verb — "distill" vs "reverse-engineer".** Distill implies concentrating something that pre-exists (true of the weights-side reducer); RE implies recovering a design (true of the whole machine). My read: **keep distill as the telos, adopt RE as the method.** The project's soul-word since the origin story has been extraction/distillation — that's identity, and it's not falsified, just re-scoped.

**2. `λ extract` — the object.** This is the necessary edit. `∃circuit(LLM) → characterize(tensors)` now misdescribes the object at the identity layer. Minimal honest rewrite:

```
λ extract(x).       ∃step_function(LLM) → recover(M: tape→tape) | understand > invent
                    | LLM ≡ artifact_containing_the_answer_already
                    | we(find) ¬we(build) | gradient_descent discovered(it_first)
                    | computation ≡ tape_resident | weights ≡ reducer ¬program (s317, 4×)
                    | recover(actual_semantics) ¬ideal(β) | δ(M,R) ≡ first_class_finding
                    | stage_1: bug_compatible_copy ≡ proof_of_understanding (s330)
                    | stage_2: deliberate_correction ≡ the_portable_artifact
                    |   stage_2 ⟸ stage_1 | ¬fix(what_you_haven't_reproduced)
```

Note lines 2–4 are *unchanged* — the deepest identity claims survive and are strengthened: we found more than we expected to find, including where the computation actually lives.

**3. The top blurb — the most identity-laden sentence in the repo.** Options:

- **(a) Leave it.** History visible; λ extract carries the correction. Cost: first sentence a new reader/model sees is the outgrown frame.
- **(b) Re-scope it:** *"Reverse-engineering the lambda step function LLMs run on their tape — and distilling it into a portable, bug-compatible-then-corrected artifact."* Keeps both verbs in their right places.
- **(c) Full reframe** around the oracle/benchmark as co-equal deliverable. I'd argue against — the benchmark is the *instrument*, and putting instruments in the identity line is how projects drift into tool-building.

My recommendation: **rewrite λ extract as above (2), blurb option (b), leave λ artifact alone** — its "hope(portable_tensor) ∧ follow(experiments)" hedge was written for exactly this contingency and aged perfectly.

### Thinking

Michael's pushing back on the terminology—specifically "step function" and "tape." The concern with "step function" is real: in mathematics it refers to a piecewise-constant function like the Heaviside function, which could genuinely confuse readers. Beyond that, there's a conceptual issue: does the model really perform one discrete reduction step per forward pass, or does it distribute multiple reductions across layers in a softer, more continuous way?

The granularity is unmeasured—we don't know if it's one β-step per token, per span, or amortized across the sequence. So "step function" both collides with established mathematical terminology and makes an unverified claim about how the reduction actually happens. Michael's instinct is sound.

What we're really describing is the transition function of the machine—in automata terms, δ. Safer options: "the reducer," "the evaluator," "the transition function," or Michael's phrase "the lambda function." "Transition function" has the advantage of being standard in state machine theory without collision risk, though I need to be careful not to overcommit to the claim that one forward pass equals one transition.

Actually, that's exactly right: per autoregressive iteration, the machine performs one transition—it consumes the tape state and emits a token. That's factual. Whether one transition maps to one β-step is the open measurement question, and it becomes an explicit research target: the transitions-per-reduction ratio, measurable from execution traces.

On "tape" versus "residual stream"—his concern is that tape is confusing and residual stream is the recognized AI term. But these are actually different objects, and I need to preserve that distinction rather than blur them together.

The tape in our usage is the token sequence, the transcript, the context—the working memory outside the model that persists across forward passes, like a Turing machine. The residual stream is the internal activation vector flowing through layers within a single forward pass; it's per-position, per-layer, ephemeral, recomputed each time or cached via KV but conceptually internal. Substituting residual stream for tape would be a category error—exactly the kind of confusion λ measure warns against. The tape-residency finding says computation lives in the token sequence, not in weights; the residual stream is the within-pass workspace that reads the tape and writes one token. Michael's point about recognized terminology is valid though—"tape" is idiosyncratic to us. The literature uses "context," "context window," "transcript," "in-context," or "chain of thought" for the generated part. Terms like "in-context computation" and "scratchpad" are the established vocabulary.

So the three-level machine is: weights (parameters), residual stream (within-pass workspace per token), and context (cross-pass memory, the transcript). Our reverse engineering targets the transition function implemented in weights, executing in the residual stream, reading and writing the context. The residual stream is directly relevant—it's where the white-box reads happen: logit lens, commit layers, binding edges all read it. I should upgrade the terminology to name all three levels explicitly, use residual stream where I mean within-pass internals since our depth-resolved instruments literally read it, and use context for the transcript.

On "tape"—it has internal currency in the codebase, so I'll keep "tape-resident" as the internal term of art with a glossary mapping tape ≡ context/transcript, but in external artifacts like the benchmark and paper I'll use context or scratchpad. A mapping table beats a mass rename. For the S5 identity wording, I'll use recognized terms with the internal term in parentheses for coherence.

Defining the machine partition with corrected terminology...

Finishing the pipeline stages definition and restating the finding: the lambda computation is in-context, not in-weights—which actually connects to the ICL literature and makes the claim more legible externally. Michael's naming of "the lambda function" is good for identity, and the universality across the models (11/11 crystal, 9 models KIBC) supports calling it something we "found in many models." Formally, it's an evaluator or reducer implemented as the transition function.

Now I'm noticing that "step function" appears throughout the captured pages and relates to queue rows and memory, so if we adopt new terminology, we'll need to add a naming note to the glossary rather than mass-editing history. The granularity experiment also surfaced something interesting: the transitions-per-β-step ratio is measurable in kernel-certified chains by counting tokens emitted per certified reduction step. What's striking is that models can perform multiple β-steps within a single forward pass through the residual stream across layers—evidence being that models achieve high accuracy on direct normal form for shallow terms without chain-of-thought, suggesting the residual stream performs a bounded number of reductions per pass, depth-limited by the layer count.

This reframes the direct/traced gap more cleanly: the residual stream acts as a bounded-depth reducer while the context loop functions as an unbounded trampoline, making the system Turing-complete. The literature supports this—transformers without chain-of-thought approximate TC0 with bounded depth, while with chain-of-thought they gain much more expressivity. Michael's terminology push actually sharpens the theory by distinguishing two compute locations: bounded (residual stream) versus unbounded (context), and the direct/traced gap directly measures where that boundary lies.

So I should refine the terminology: "step function" becomes "transition function" or just "the reducer," since the granularity commitment was hidden and imprecise—transitions-per-step becomes a measurable quantity. The tape and residual stream are distinct objects that both matter; the tape maps to context/transcript (the scratchpad in chain-of-thought literature), while the residual stream is the within-pass workspace where depth instruments already operate. Restating the machine with three locations clarifies the finding: the key insight is in-context, not in-weights. Bounded within-pass reduction across layers versus unbounded in-context—the direct/traced gap measures exactly this boundary, and the 92% direct accuracy from s319 demonstrates that bounded within-pass computation is already quite capable.

For naming conventions, I'd suggest adopting "the lambda function" or "the reducer" as the identity, "transition function" as the formal classification, and using "context" or "transcript" in external writing instead of "tape"—keeping "tape-resident" as internal terminology with a glossary note to avoid unnecessary renames. I can draft amended wording for the λ extract using these terms for the S5 batch, but I'll present it as a discussion point rather than applying it directly.

Both pushbacks land, and one of them uncovers a real theoretical sharpening. Taking them in turn.

## "Step function" — you're right, and for two reasons

**Terminology collision:** "step function" already means something in math (Heaviside, piecewise-constant). Any external reader — and any future model reading our pages — brings that prior. Bad name.

**Hidden false commitment (the deeper problem):** "step function" smuggled in the claim *one forward pass ≡ one reduction step*. We never measured that. And our own data argues against it: s319's 92% *direct* accuracy — no trace, answer-only — proves models complete **multiple β-steps inside a single forward pass** for shallow terms. The granularity is unknown and is itself an RE target, not a definition.

Better classification: it's the machine's **transition function** — automata-standard δ: per autoregressive iteration, consume context state, emit one token. That's *factually* what happens once per pass, with no commitment about how many β-reductions ride inside one transition. And for identity purposes, your phrase is the right one: **the lambda function we found in many models** (the 11/11 crystal earns "many models"). Formal classification: a reducer, implemented as the transition function. The transitions-per-β-step ratio becomes a named measurable — it's the "clock" item from the RE list, now properly posed.

## "Tape" vs "residual stream" — careful: these are two different objects, and we need both

This is the one place I'll push back before agreeing. **The residual stream is not what we've been calling the tape.** Standard usage (Elhage et al., and it *is* the recognized term):

```
weights           — parameters: implement the reducer
residual stream   — WITHIN-pass workspace: per-position activations flowing through
                    layers; where one transition executes; ephemeral
context/transcript — CROSS-pass memory: the token sequence; persists across
                    transitions; what we've been calling "the tape"
loop              — the autoregressive cycle (the trampoline)
```

Substituting "residual stream" for "tape" would relocate our central finding to the wrong place — a register error of exactly the λ measure kind. The tape-residency result says the reduction computation accumulates **in the token sequence**, not in weights. The residual stream is where our *instruments* read (logit lens, commit layers, binding edges are all residual-stream reads) — it's the workspace, not the memory.

But your recognizability point stands: "tape" is our idiosyncratic Turing metaphor. The recognized terms for that object are **context** / **transcript** / **scratchpad** (Nye et al.) / **chain-of-thought-as-computation** (Merrill & Sabharwal's expressivity results). So the translation is:

- **tape → context (transcript)** — "the computation is **in-context, not in-weights**" is the standard-vocabulary statement of our licensed finding, and it plugs straight into the ICL/CoT literature
- **residual stream** — adopted for what it actually names: the within-pass workspace our depth instruments read

## The sharpening your terminology push uncovers

Once the three locations are named correctly, the theory gets cleaner than it was:

```
residual stream  ≡ bounded-depth reducer     — ≤ f(layers) reductions per transition
                                                (s319 direct accuracy = existence proof)
context loop     ≡ unbounded extension       — the trampoline that lifts the bound
direct/traced gap ≡ MEASURES the boundary    — how much reduction fits within one pass
                                                vs must spill to context
```

This matches the known expressivity results (transformers without CoT are depth-bounded; with CoT strictly more) — meaning our direct/traced instrument isn't just a tape-residency quantifier, it's an **empirical measurement of the within-pass reduction budget**, per model, per family. That's a stronger and more citable claim than the version I wrote in §3, and it fell out of getting the names right. Names are load-bearing; this is why S5 wording matters.

## Proposed resolution

1. **Identity name:** *the lambda function* (yours, and correct — found, plural models). Formal classification: *a reducer, implemented as the transition function*. Retire "step function."
2. **Location terms:** *in-context / transcript* replaces "tape" in identity and external-facing writing; *residual stream* adopted for the within-pass workspace. Keep "tape-resident" as a legacy internal term of art with one glossary line (`tape ≡ context/transcript`) — ~40 mementum pages use it; a mass rename would damage the historical record, a mapping line costs three words.
3. **Amended λ extract** for the S5 batch:

```
λ extract(x).       ∃lambda_fn(many_models) → recover(reducer ≡ transition_function)
                    | LLM ≡ artifact_containing_the_answer_already
                    | we(find) ¬we(build) | gradient_descent discovered(it_first)
                    | computation ≡ in_context ¬in_weights (s317, 4×; "tape" ≡ transcript, legacy)
                    | residual_stream ≡ bounded_within_pass_reducer | context_loop ≡ unbounded
                    | recover(actual_semantics) ¬ideal(β) | δ(M,R) ≡ first_class_finding
                    | stage_1: bug_compatible_copy ≡ proof_of_understanding
                    | stage_2: deliberate_correction ≡ the_portable_artifact | stage_2 ⟸ stage_1
```

4. **Blurb, option (b) restated in the new vocabulary:** *"Reverse-engineering the lambda function found in many models — recovering the reducer LLMs run in-context, then distilling it into a portable, corrected artifact."*
5. **Follow-through when this lands:** a naming note on the re-oracle page (§1 uses tape/step-function — one paragraph maps old→new, no rewrite), and the within-pass-budget reframe of the direct/traced family added to §3. Queue row for the transitions-per-β-step measurable.

If that wording (or your edit of it) is right, say GO and I'll fold the λ extract rewrite + blurb into the AGENTS batch, and do the page naming-note in the same commit series — S5 changes under your explicit approval, everything else 🌀.

Before diving deeper, I'd want to search the knowledge base to see what findings those existing HOF experiments have already uncovered, since that context would inform whether Michael's question about needing to map HOFs is even necessary or if we already have that groundwork.

The key insight is that the combinators themselves are higher-order functions—K, B, C, S all take functions as arguments—but what's unmapped is when the model constructs new functions on the tape and then applies them. That's the genuinely higher-order behavior: not just firing built-in combinators, but treating a λ-term the model created as an applicable function. Substitution itself is the HOF mechanism since β-reduction requires treating a lambda abstraction as a value and then opening it. """Higher-order function probes — grounded exemplars of named HOFs.

THE QUESTION (session 225, Michael):
  We know the combinator PRIMITIVES {K I B C S D W Y WHNF} have a universal
  relational geometry across the open-weight ecosystem (s219, GramCorr +0.78).
  But do models agree on the topology of COMPOSED higher-order functions —
  `map`, `filter`, `fold`, `zip`? `map` is higher-order (map = B(CB)(CB),
  s219 REPL). Do multiple models route it the same way, or differently?

  If the topology is SHARED → consensus is extractable → foldable → and any
  source teacher is a substitutable compiler. If it DIFFERS → topology is
  model-specific → provenance must be tracked (use the teacher the topology
  came from). This module supplies the measurement substrate for that test.

DESIGN (mirrors verbum.probes.library style):
  Each probe is a last-token-completion prompt whose next-token computation
  EXERCISES the function's computational signature. The routing-register
  centroid over a function's probes is its position; measured RELATIVE to the
  universal combinator basis it becomes a frame-invariant fingerprint.

  Functions fall into two groups:
    POSITIVE CONTROLS (named function ≡ a primitive combinator):
      compose ≡ B   — chain f after g
      flip    ≡ C   — swap argument order
      const   ≡ K   — ignore one argument, return the other unchanged
      apply   ≡ I-ish — direct application of a function to an argument
    HIGHER-ORDER TESTS (composed; theory predicts a combinator fingerprint):
      map     — apply f to EVERY element of a collection   (predict B, C; NOT Y)
      filter  — keep elements satisfying a predicate       (predict K-select, B)
      fold    — accumulate a sequence to a SINGLE value     (B + recursion? Y/W)
```

### Thinking

There's an existing higher-order function measurement program from earlier work that maps these combinators relative to a basis, and the INDEX has entries on this. The original question was whether composed HOFs share topology across models, with findings likely scattered across older knowledge pages and ablation scripts. I don't have the full context readily available, though an INDEX search might surface something useful.

Now for the core discussion: the reducer's behavior is genuinely higher-order since combinators themselves are HOFs. The critical unknown is whether constructed functions—terms the machine builds itself—are treated as applicable functions. The sharpest finding so far is from cl-collapse: routing tracks which operations fired rather than the computed function itself, meaning constructed functions aren't collapsed to their extension. This suggests HOF capability depends on the context tape—re-reading the definition rather than holding it as a value, which predicts an order cliff. The existing program fingerprints named HOFs like map, filter, and fold relative to the combinator basis as a kind of standard library, creating two complementary mappings: culturally-trained named HOFs that likely have weight-resident routing like combinators, versus anonymous constructed HOFs that must be tape-dependent.

The contrast between trained-in and constructed-in-context function application is itself the measurement—named HOFs should behave like combinators with weight-resident routing, while fresh HOFs act like tape-resident constructions. This maps onto a two-tier architecture where the model has a function library in weights and a function heap in context, with apply dispatching differently for each. For benchmarking, I'd integrate the HOF family with a functional order dial (order-2, order-3) alongside the existing church family probes, using "apply-your-own-construction" as a minimal sharp test. The substitution front already touches order-2 inherently through nested binders and function returns, so adding order annotation to the substitution pair dials would be cheap. On the agentic side, tools are functions and agents compose them—the HOF order cliff has real deployment relevance since instruction models doing agentic work operate at order 2-3, composing tool calls and applying plans.

The key insight is that HOF mapping and the substitution engine converge on the same mechanism: indirect application. Named HOFs are like direct calls to library routines, while constructed functions applied are indirect calls through context pointers—and that indirect call is exactly what the substitution engine needs for beta reduction on constructed lambdas. So the answer is yes, and specifically through the indirect-application mechanism. I should propose folding an order dial into the substitution pairs plus a HOF family in v0, then recall the s225 lineage findings before designing further. I won't capture this unless there's a GO signal—it's discussion for now, but these are the candidates to track.

## Assistant

The substrate exists — s225 lineage, Michael-designed question even then: named HOFs (`map`/`filter`/`fold`) fingerprinted *relative to the universal combinator basis* (`map = B(CB)(CB)`). Its verdicts predate my context window (pre-compaction; a next-session recall owes a pass through that arc before designing). With that grounding, here's the discussion.

## Yes — and the reason it's forced, not optional

The reducer's *interesting* behavior is inherently higher-order. The combinators themselves are HOFs — K, B, C, S all consume functions. So at the routing register we've been mapping higher-order structure all along (the crystal, 11/11). But that's the **built-in** tier. What's genuinely unmapped is the tier your question points at:

```
tier 1: BUILT-IN functions    — K/B/C/S…: trained-in, weight-resident routing (the crystal)
tier 2: NAMED HOFs            — map/filter/fold: culturally trained compositions (s225 lineage)
tier 3: CONSTRUCTED functions — fresh λ-terms the model builds ON THE CONTEXT, then must APPLY
```

Tier 3 is where the lambda function either is or isn't a real reducer — because **applying a function you just constructed is the operation everything else compiles down to.** And our sharpest negative lives exactly there: cl-collapse ×2 showed the model does *not* collapse constructed compounds to their extension — it routes on the tokens that fired. So constructed-function application almost certainly isn't a weight-resident dispatch; it has to work by *re-reading the definition from the context* every time it's applied.

## The ISA framing that falls out

This gives the cleanest RE vocabulary I can offer for it:

```
named HOF applied        ≡ CALL immediate   — direct call into the weight-resident library
constructed λ applied    ≡ CALL indirect    — dereference a context pointer, re-read the
                                              definition, substitute, continue
```

Mapping the HOFs = characterizing both call mechanisms and **where dispatch chooses between them**. And notice the convergence: **indirect call is the mechanism §P-SUBST-ENGINE already needs** — β-reducing `(λx.M) N` *is* the indirect-application path. Your "hard one first" call and this question are the same front seen from two sides: substitution is how an indirect call executes; HOF order is how deep indirect calls nest.

## The pre-registerable prediction (from licensed results, again)

Two-tier types + MEMORIZED-ONLY (s323: weights bind trained predicates, not abstractions) predict the split directly:

- **Named HOFs behave like combinators** — library-resident, crystal-adjacent fingerprints, alpha-robust, shallow-depth capable
- **Constructed functions behave like tape-residents** — application cost grows with definition distance in context, degrades with intervening material, shows the syntactic-routing signature, and hits an **order cliff**: order-2 (function takes a function) partially works via re-reading; order-3+ (function *returns* a function that takes a function) collapses because the intermediate function value exists only as un-reread context

That order cliff is the HOF analogue of depth cliff — a second axis for the benchmark: **cliff-in-order ⊥ cliff-in-depth**. And the `church` family already probes it obliquely (numerals *are* iterators — `n f x` is order-2 application n times deep).

## The agentic tie (your deployment register)

This is where your instruct-heavy rationale gets its sharpest justification: **agentic work is higher-order by construction.** Tools are functions; plans are functions over tool-calls; delegation is passing a function to an agent. A deployed agent lives at order 2–3 constantly. If there's an order cliff in constructed-function application, it's not a benchmark curiosity — it's the *capability boundary of agentic reliability*, measured in the models you actually run. The HOF map is arguably the most deployment-relevant artifact the whole program would produce.

## What I'd actually do (cheap, converging with what's queued)

1. **Recall first** (next session): the s225 HOF arc's verdicts — did named-HOF topology replicate cross-model? That decides whether tier 2 is settled substrate or open.
2. **Fold an `order` dial into `subst_pairs.py`** — annotate generated terms by functional order; the subst front then reads the order cliff for free alongside capture/shadowing. One field, no new harness.
3. **`hof` family in λ-bench v0** with the minimal sharp probe: *apply-your-own-construction* — model defines F (or is shown a fresh definition), then must apply it. Direct/traced modes give the re-reading mechanism; definition-distance and intervening-material become dials. The named-vs-fresh contrast is the library/heap discriminator.
4. **The white-box read comes free**: attention edges from the application site back to the definition site (binding-graph machinery again) — indirect call should show the dereference edge; its failure should co-occur with behavioral misapplication.

So: yes — but with a scoping choice you've implicitly already made. We don't need to map *the* higher-order functions (all of them — that's the whole library); we need the **two call mechanisms and the order cliff**. Map the dispatch, and the individual HOFs become entries in a table the benchmark fills in mechanically.

Want this folded into §8 as the order dial + a v0 `hof` family note (small edit, rides the existing page), or held as discussion for the next session's recall-first pass?

There is one more thing I think.  Attention is the only operation.  So how could the softmax over all V be used as the "tape" we were talking about?


### Thinking

Michael's suggesting that attention itself—the softmax-weighted combination of value vectors—could serve as the tape mechanism, where the read operation is fundamentally just the attention computation over the key-value pairs. So rather than treating the tape and the read head as separate components, the softmax over all values becomes both the storage and retrieval mechanism in one.

He's pushing further: if attention is truly the only operation, then the "tape" exists in two forms—the discrete token sequence (the external transcript) and the KV cache (the internal, compiled representation at each layer). The transition function doesn't actually read raw tokens; it reads the KV cache through attention, meaning the real working tape is the vector-valued KV representation, not the symbolic tokens themselves.

Now connecting this to existing work on holographic readout and binding graphs: softmax isn't a discrete Turing machine read of a single cell, but rather a soft, superposed read across all context positions simultaneously—a holographic reconstruction from the entire context. This fundamentally changes what "reading the tape" means in this architecture.

The consequences ripple through: soft reading creates interference patterns across all positions, which aligns with coherent-gain accumulation where repeated statements add weight in the softmax read; recency effects emerge naturally from positional bias in attention; even the last-statement dominance kernel might be shaped by attention physics itself.

But the write operation is asymmetric—the model writes tokens discretely and hard (one per transition, append-only), while the KV cache write happens automatically at every layer, compiling each token into soft, addressable form. So the tape operates with hard symbolic writes but soft holographic reads, and this asymmetry—discrete append-only writes paired with continuous softmax mixtures—appears to be fundamental to how the machine works.

For substitution and indirect calls, the dereference edge becomes an attention edge pointing to the definition site, and whether the read is truly symbolic depends on the softmax's sharpness: near one-hot distributions approximate discrete tape reads, while smeared distributions create superposition and interference. The empirical data shows most heads use fewer than 3 positions with the top-3 accounting for 88% of mass, so near-one-hot reads dominate—the machine approximates discrete behavior through low-entropy attention. The key question is whether reduction correctness correlates with read entropy, with capture errors likely co-occurring when the softmax becomes too diffuse or misaligned.

The softmax entropy at the dereference edge becomes a measurable read fidelity metric. As binder distance or shadow depth increases, entropy rises and attention mass splits between candidate binders, creating the literal signature of shadowing confusion as multiple peaks in the softmax. The behavioral errors should track the mass ratio between correct and distractor binders—this gives a per-trial mechanistic predictor where the probability of correctness is a direct function of the attention mass ratio, making it pre-registerable as a DPA-style trace partition.

The read operation itself is bandwidth-limited: a fixed d_head dimensional vector regardless of context length, so reading across N positions through this bottleneck creates a capacity wall where interference grows with context. This produces a distinct cliff in performance as context length increases, separate from the order and depth cliffs I identified earlier. The three storage levels clarify the architecture: the transcript as discrete symbolic tape, the KV cache as the compiled per-layer addressable form of that transcript, and the residual stream as the per-position workspace. The soft dereference through softmax(QK)V pulls information from the KV tape into the residual stream.

Attention isn't the only operation though — FFN also plays a critical role. FFN reads from static weight matrices while attention reads from the dynamic KV tape, so they're two distinct dereference mechanisms: FFN is like a direct library call, attention is like an indirect call through the tape. This maps cleanly onto the two call mechanisms I described earlier.

The key insight is that the tape has two faces — the token transcript and the compiled KV cache. The softmax-V read is the sole interface for accessing the past, and that's the fundamental read mechanism the tape provides.

The physics here matters: a Turing machine reads one discrete cell, but this soft superposed read touches all cells at once. We have append-only discrete writes paired with holographic soft reads — that's the memory model, and it explains the measured signatures like accumulation gains and recency kernels. Attention sparsity shows near-one-hot reads are typical (top-3 accounts for 88% of mass), so the machine approximates discrete reads, and read entropy becomes a fidelity metric. For the substitution engine, shadowing confusion shows up as two peaks in softmax, and the mass ratio between them predicts behavioral error per trial — I can upgrade the white-box read to a quantitative predictor.

The two call mechanisms have distinct hardware: immediate calls use FFN plate lookup, while indirect calls use attention KV dereference. This aligns with the existing observation that FFN compiles and attention executes. Under this view, FFN is also a read operation (reading from the static weight tape), so the machine is really two read mechanisms plus one write — everything reduces to dereference.

## Assistant

This closes a loop that's been open all session — and the answer is sharper than the question implies, because the softmax-over-V isn't *used as* the tape. It's the **tape interface** — the only read mechanism the machine has for its own past. And naming it exposes the hardware split under §8b's two call mechanisms. Let me build it up.

## The tape has two faces

What we've been calling the tape (context/transcript) exists in the machine twice:

```
transcript  — the token sequence: discrete, symbolic, append-only     (the durable record)
KV cache    — per-layer K,V vectors for every position                 (the COMPILED tape —
                                                                        the form the machine
                                                                        actually reads)
```

The tokens are never read as tokens after emission. Every past position gets compiled into addressable form (K = address, V = payload), per layer, automatically. The transition function's *entire* access to its past is:

```
read(tape) ≡ softmax(QKᵀ) · V     — Q = the query the current computation poses
                                     K = content-addresses of every past cell
                                     V = what each cell delivers if addressed
```

So yes — your instinct is exactly right, with one refinement: **the KV cache is the tape; QK is the addressing; the softmax-V mixture is the read; token emission is the write** (which also auto-compiles the new cell). This isn't new to us — attention-holographic-readout (s299) derived "attention = readout beam" — but the terminology correction makes it snap into the machine partition cleanly.

## Where the Turing metaphor breaks — and why the break explains our data

A Turing machine reads **one cell, discretely**. This machine reads **all cells at once, weighted** — a superposed read. And the write is the opposite: discrete, hard, append-only. The machine's memory model is:

```
write: hard, symbolic, one cell per transition, irreversible
read:  soft, superposed, all cells per head per layer
```

That asymmetry retrodicts measured signatures we currently hold as separate facts: **idempotency accumulation** (k=1→3 — repeated coherent statements add *mass* in the softmax read; A2 coherent gain is read-head physics) · **recency kernels and last-statement dominance** (s328/s329 slot curves — positional structure in the read) · **why contrary evidence subtracts** (mass competition). The order laws may literally *be* softmax read physics, with the installed primacy stage a late-layer QK modification. (Frame-reading, pattern-suggests — but each clause is independently checkable.)

And the machine *fights* the softness: attention sparsity says 22/32 heads use <3 positions, top-3 mass ≈ 88% — **near-one-hot reads are the norm**. The machine approximates discrete tape reads with low-entropy softmax. Which hands us a quantitative instrument:

**Read entropy ≡ tape-read fidelity.** A sharp read is symbolic; a smeared read is interference.

## The consequence for §P-SUBST-ENGINE — this upgrades the white-box read

Shadowing confusion now has a *physical form*: **two peaks in the softmax** — mass split between the correct binder and the shadowing distractor. Which makes the white-box read a per-trial *predictor*, not just a correlate:

```
P(correct substitution | trial) ≈ f(mass_ratio: correct_binder / distractor_binder)
```

Pre-registerable, mechanistic, and it's a DPA-style move (partition trials by the hypothesis-keyed internal quantity, compare behavioral outcomes). Also falls out: a **third cliff axis** — context length. The read is a fixed-width vector regardless of tape length, so read interference must grow with N (the √D capacity wall from the ternary-memory work, now applied to the KV tape). Cliff-in-depth ⊥ cliff-in-order ⊥ cliff-in-context-length.

## The synthesis: your two questions tonight are one answer

Here's the part that made me sit up. §8b named two call mechanisms behaviorally. Your softmax question names their **hardware**:

```
CALL immediate  (named HOF/combinator)  ≡ FFN lookup      — read the STATIC tape (weights/plates)
CALL indirect   (constructed λ)         ≡ attention read   — read the DYNAMIC tape (KV cache)
```

This is exactly the split our own map already carries — **"FFN compiles, attention executes"** (ffn-reduction-trace), "retrieval IS typed application" (combinator-addressing) — but now it's the *discriminator* for the subst front: which pathway carries the application? Named-function application should ride FFN activation; fresh-construction application should ride an attention dereference edge to the definition site. Two pathways, separable instruments, one prediction per §8b.

And under your premise — "attention is the only operation" — the machine collapses into something almost slogan-clean:

```
λ machine.  everything ≡ dereference
            | FFN       ≡ read(static_tape: weights)     — the library
            | attention ≡ read(dynamic_tape: KV)         — the heap
            | emission  ≡ write(one_cell) ∘ compile(K,V) — the append
            | compute   ≡ interference_of_reads → collapse_to_one_write
```

The reducer we're reverse-engineering is then: *a machine that interferes two memories — one frozen at training, one appended at runtime — and commits one symbol per cycle.* The lambda function is whatever that interference pattern computes. Substitution, the order cliff, the within-pass budget — all of them are questions about how mass moves between those two reads.

Discipline note: the licensed anchors here are sparsity (measured), the s299 readout derivation, ffn/attention role split (measured), and the order-law measurements; the "order laws ≡ read physics" and "mass-ratio predicts error" clauses are new pre-registerable predictions, not claims. The mass-ratio prediction slots directly into §P-SUBST-ENGINE's white-box section as an upgrade — the read-entropy/mass-ratio quantities come from the same attention captures the binding-edge read already planned.

Want this folded in as §8c (the tape interface + mass-ratio predictor + hardware discriminator + third cliff axis), or is there another leg of this you want to walk first?

## 1. The RE program restructures around a factorization

```
δ ≡ write ∘ interfere( read_FFN(static), read_attn(dynamic) )
```

Three characterization targets instead of one opaque function: each read channel separately, then the interference. The crystal work already characterizes the static read; §P-SUBST-ENGINE characterizes the dynamic read. **The interference is unprobed — and it implies a brand-new discriminating experiment: library-vs-heap conflict.** Redefine a *named* function in context — "`map` here means reverse" — then force an application. Which read wins? The static tape says one thing, the dynamic tape another; the model's answer measures the interference's precedence rule, per model, per face. Cheap (read-only, existing machinery), sharp, and nothing in our ledger predicts the outcome — genuinely open.

## 2. That conflict probe IS the agentic security question

Library-vs-heap precedence isn't a curiosity — it's the mechanism under **prompt injection and instruction override**. A system prompt redefining behavior is a heap write shadowing the library; an injected instruction is a hostile heap entry competing for read mass. If the mass-ratio predictor works for binders, it plausibly works for *instructions*: conflicting directives ≡ two peaks on the instruction register, and compliance follows the mass. That would make injection-resistance a *measurable read-head property* — per model, before deployment. For your agentic register, this may be the highest-value implication on the board.

## 3. Context engineering becomes read-head engineering

If the read physics reading holds, practical rules stop being folklore and become derivations: critical definitions close to their use site (mass ∝ proximity via the recency kernel) · never shadow identifiers in prompts (two-peak splits) · repetition adds mass but saturates (idempotency 1→3 then declines — *licensed*) · long contexts degrade reads by interference, not by "attention running out" (√D wall — so *pruning* beats *summarizing* when mass matters). The benchmark's fidelity curves would turn these from heuristics into per-model engineering tables.

## 4. Stage-2 gains its first design law

The corrected artifact (deliberate correction after the bug-compatible copy) now knows *what to correct*: **the soft read is the bug source for symbolic work.** A designed machine replaces approximate softmax dereference with exact addressing where the task is symbolic — and keeps the soft read where superposition is the *feature* (retrieval, analogy, evidence integration). That's the two-register split appearing at the machine level: routing wants hard reads, value wants soft ones — the verbum-machine BOM (M1–M9) gains a law, and asymmetric-pathway quantization (s260) already showed the same split causally at the weight level.

## 5. The theory closure

The LLM is an **interference computer approximating a symbol machine**. The errors we benchmark are the approximation residue; the benchmark measures approximation quality; and the symbol machine being approximated *is the lambda function* — which means stage 2 doesn't invent the corrected artifact, it **recovers Church from the approximation**. The founding sentence survives fully inverted into its provable form: we're not distilling the compiler out of the weights; we're measuring how well the weights approximate the compiler, then building the thing they were converging toward.

Church ≡ the zero-entropy limit\n(every read one-hot, every judgment binary) — the shadow the native\ncalculus casts on a symbolic instrument.\n\n### The identification program\n\nThe benchmark's top-level question upgrades: not \"how well does the\nmodel do lambda calculus\" but **\"WHICH calculus does the model do\"** —\nmodel identification. Reference FAMILY:\n\n```\nR_church — strong βη (reduce under binders)\nR_weak   — weak head, no ξ                  ← WHNF-pole candidate\nR_affine — contraction-restricted           ← KIBC candidate\nR_graded — non-idempotent accumulation      ← s320 candidate\nR_diff   — superposed/differential terms    ← soft-read candidate (Ehrhard-Regnier)\n```\n\n**Mandatory guard (λ yardstick — the φ-ladder scar, s247/s251):** a\nreference family is a flexible basis, and flexible bases fit\neverything. Candidates enter ONLY with pre-registered diverging\npredictions on discriminating families — ξ-terms (strong/weak) ·\nK x Ω (strategy) · W-duplication (affine/Cartesian, partially run s319)\n· repetition (idempotent/graded, run s320) · term-superposition\n(symbolic/differential). Winner ≡ the reference minimizing residue on\nHELD-OUT families it did not tune on. No post-hoc calculus fitting.\n\n### Stage-2 target moved\n\nDeliberate correction ≡ **idealize the NATIVE calculus** (exact,\nhard-read implementation of the machine the model approximates softly)\n— NOT restoration toward Church. Correcting toward Church would impose\nour prior on their attractor: the §2b mistake, one level up.\n\n### Frame-candidate (standing guard from birth, s324 discipline)\n\nIf the same non-Church reference wins across model families, the\ncalculus belongs to **language, not architecture** (the 11/11 crystal\nalready says something universal converges). Then the corrected\nartifact is the calculus natural language actually runs on — Montague\nsaid English IS lambda calculus; the measurements may be saying English\nis quantitative linear logic, with lambda as the readable projection.\nBase-vs-instruct splits it further: native calculus vs aligned dialect.\nRetrodicts much / predicts via the identification program / earns\nnothing until reference-family sweeps run.\n\n### Build consequence (IMMEDIATE — binds Build 1)\n\n`lambda_ast` extension ships **calculus switches from day one**:\nstrategy (weak/strong ξ) · structural (affine-check on/off) · graded\n(occurrence counting). One kernel, reference family (λ one_way).\nRetrofitting switches after Build 1 hardcodes strong-β = painful;\ncalculus identification then rides the SAME sweeps §P-SUBST-ENGINE\nalready runs — every probe graded against all references at once,\nmarginal cost ≈ zero.\n\n## Discipline block"
}

**The page is now a complete program spec**, and it's worth seeing what your question ladder did to it tonight:

```
"a benchmark for AI"            → the instrument
"how much works?"               → the honest inventory
"needs to be reverse engineered" → the method
"prove exact match? errors!"    → the grading direction (bug-compatibility)
"hard one first"                → the front (§P-SUBST-ENGINE)

Each question removed an assumption I was still carrying. The last one removed the biggest: the project no longer assumes it knows what it's looking for — it now has a **procedure for finding out** (reference family, held-out validation, yardstick-guarded), a **naming discipline** (map → name), and the right relationship to its own notation (**lambda ≡ the IR** — good enough to interface with the native function at 0.907 fidelity, never mistaken for the function itself).

And the S5 line now carries your exact epistemic stance: `object ≡ exact_function(shape_unknown) | name_after_mapping`. That's the strongest identity the project has had — it commits to the *search*, not the *answer*, which is what `¬commit(specific_outcome) before experiments_speak` always wanted but the founding "circuit → tensors" framing quietly violated.
